## Page 1


ZERO-FREE REGIONS AND CONCENTRATION INEQUALITIES FOR HYPERGRAPH
COLORINGS IN THE LOCAL LEMMA REGIME
JINGCHENG LIU, YIXIAO YU
AbstRact. We show that for 𝑞-colorings in 𝑘-uniform hypergraphs with maximum degree Δ, if 𝑘≥50 and
𝑞≥700Δ
5
𝑘−10 , there is a “Lee-Yang” zero-free strip around the interval [0, 1] of the partition function, which
includes the special case of uniform enumeration of hypergraph colorings. As an immediate consequence,
we obtain Berry-Esseen type inequalities for hypergraph 𝑞-colorings under such conditions, demonstrating
the asymptotic normality for the size of any color class in a uniformly random coloring. Our framework also
extends to the study of “Fisher zeros”, leading to deterministic algorithms for approximating the partition
function in the zero-free region.
Our approach is based on extending the recent work of [Liu, Wang, Yin, Yu, STOC 2025] to general con-
straint satisfaction problems (CSP). We focus on partition functions defined for CSPs by introducing external
fields to the variables. A key component in our approach is a projection-lifting scheme, which enables us
to essentially lift information percolation type analysis for Markov chains from the real line to the complex
plane. Last but not least, we also show a Chebyshev-type inequality under the sampling LLL condition for
atomic CSPs.

1. IntRoduction
The location of complex zeros of partition functions is intimately connected to the study of phase
transitions in statistical physics, dating back to the famous Lee-Yang program [LY52]. Since then, it
has also found applications in combinatorics [HL72, Wag09], Chernoff bounds [KS18], asymptotic nor-
mality [God81a], central limit theorems [LPRS16, MS24, JPSS22], and in designing efficient approximate
counting algorithms [Bar16, PR17, LSS17]. Establishing a zero-free region has therefore attracted a lot of
attention, and tight zero-free results around the real axis have been obtained for numerous graph polyno-
mials, notably the independent set polynomial [SS03, PR19] and monomer-dimer model [HL72]. The case
of hypergraphs is much less understood, especially for spin systems on 𝑘-uniform hypergraphs.
Hypergraph 𝑞-colorings, introduced by Erdős [Erd63], have long been one of the most fruitful testbeds
in probabilistic combinatorics. To name a few, they featured in the Lovász local lemma (LLL) [EL73], the
nibble technique [Röd85], the container method [ST15, BMS18], and frequented the textbook by Alon and
Spencer [AS16]. A standard application of LLL shows that for 𝑘-uniform hypergraphs with maximum
degree Δ, there is a proper 𝑞-coloring as soon as 𝑞> 𝐶Δ
1
𝑘−1 for some constant 𝐶, and such coloring
can be found by efficient algorithms [Bec91, Mos09, MT10]. For simple hypergraphs (two hyperedges
can share at most one vertex), Frieze et al. [FM11, FA17] showed that standard Glauber dynamics mixes
rapidly provided that 𝑞≥max
n
𝐶𝑘ln𝑛, 500𝑘3Δ
1
𝑘−1
o
. For non-simple hypergraphs, however, the space of 𝑞-
colorings can become disconnected [FM11], causing Glauber dynamics to fail. Such a connectivity barrier
is not unique to hypergraph coloring but appears broadly in CSPs under local lemma like conditions.
More recently, there is a beautiful line of work studying a counting/sampling variant of LLL for CSPs.
Formally, one considers a uniformly random assignment to the CSP: let 𝑝be the maximum violation
probability over all constraints, and 𝐷be the maximum degree of the dependency graph (the number
of constraints that share variables with a given constraint). While the classical LLL [EL73, She85] says
that the satisfiability threshold is asymptotically e𝑝𝐷≤1, numerous exciting recent developments sug-
gest that 𝑝𝐷𝑐≲1 for some constant 𝑐≥2 would be sufficient so that uniform sampling of solutions
under LLL can be done efficiently. Examples such as hypergraph independent sets [HSZ19, QWZ22], 𝑘-
CNF [Moi19, FGYZ21a], hypergraph 𝑞-colorings [GLLZ18], random CNF formulas [GGGHP22, HWY23b],
as well as general CSP instances [FHY21, JPV21a, JPV21b, HSW21, FGW+23], have been considered. Two
notable cases are𝑘-CNF and hypergraph𝑞-colorings, representative for boolean CSP and for CSPs with suf-
ficiently large domains, respectively. A recent breakthrough result by Wang and Yin [WY24] established
the sampling LLL under the condition 𝑝𝐷2+𝑜𝑞(1) ≲1 for domain size 𝑞. This is asymptotically optimal for
large enough 𝑞, as NP-hardness has been shown by [BGG+19, GGW23] for 𝑝𝐷2 ≳1.
In this paper, we study “Lee-Yang zeros” of the hypergraph 𝑞-coloring partition functions in the sam-
pling LLL regime. An interesting prospect from establishing zero-free regions is the concentration phe-
nomenon and central limit theorems. Specifically, in the case of hypergraph 𝑞-colorings, we are interested
in the size of any color class under a uniformly random coloring. To some extent, one could view the LLL
conditions as a relaxation of the independence assumption, but does the bounded dependence structure of
LLL give rise to concentration? Near the existence threshold of e𝑝𝐷≤1, concentration would be rare to
find, because many random CSPs exhibit a rigid phenomenon with high probability near the satisfiability
threshold, where nearly all the variables are frozen (see, e.g., [ACO08]). We show that under the stronger
condition of 𝑝𝐷2+𝑜𝑞(1) ≲1/𝑞, a Chebyshev type inequality can still be derived for atomic CSPs: in a uni-
formly random satisfying assignment, the size of any color class lies within 1 ±𝑂( 1
𝑛) times its expectation.
This leads to a natural question, can one also prove central limit theorems (CLT) and normal approxima-
tions in similar sampling LLL regimes? CLTs under various local dependence structures have also been
studied before. Indeed, if one is only concerned about the number of violated constraints in a random as-
signment, then Stein’s method would already suffice (e.g., [CS04, CHA08]). To obtain a CLT for the size of
color classes, we follow a different route through complex zero-freeness [LPRS16, MS24, JPSS22, LWYY25].
Specifically, we introduce “external fields” to the variables of a CSP to form a partition function and show
1


## Page 3


that in a complex-plane neighborhood of the point 1, the partition function does not vanish. Then, non-
asymptotic central limit theorems (CLT) and local central limit theorems can be derived analytically in a
neighborhood of 1. CLT and local CLT are desirable in many applications as they combine both concen-
tration aspects (the random variable does not deviate too much from the mean) and anti-concentration
aspects (the random variable is non-degenerate and can still fluctuate like a Gaussian). We also mention
in passing that while concentration bounds for the number of resampling steps in the Moser-Tardos algo-
rithm have also been established [KS11, AI16, HH17], these do not seem to translate to the concentration
of the LLL distribution itself in general, unless for extremal LLL instances.
Classical techniques for establishing zero-free regions include the Lee-Yang approach [LY52] and its
extensions such as [Asa70, Rue71], as well as the contraction method [PR19, LSS19, SS19] based on self-
avoiding walk tree construction [God81b, SS03] and the decay of correlation [Wei06, BG08]. More recently,
there have also been several works extending these approaches beyond pairwise interactions. Notably,
[Bar24] studied Fisher zeros under many-body soft interactions, where one relaxes hard constraints and
introduces a “penalty factor” for every violated constraint instead. Then, one studies complex zeros in
terms of the “penalty factor” around the point 1. Partition functions in terms of Fisher zeros are better
suited for studying optimization landscapes in Max-CSPs as opposed to solutions in CSPs. This is mainly
due to relaxing with a “penalty factor” around the point 1 (corresponding to “almost no interactions”), and
it is unclear how to extract useful information about the solution space of the original CSP (corresponding
to “maximum interactions”). To encode the solutions of the original CSP, the standard approach is to
consider the Lee-Yang zeros, where an external field is introduced to the variables. Then, a neighborhood
around the point 1 still corresponds to “almost-uniform random distribution over CSP solutions”. Two
notable works along the same line concern the hypergraph independent set models. [GMP+24] studied
zero-free disk mainly around the origin, while [LWYY25] showed a zero-free strip around the real interval
[0, 1] under the asymptotically optimal sampling LLL condition 𝑝𝐷2 ≲1. Notably, [LWYY25] introduced
the framework of complex extensions of the Markov chain for locating complex zeros of the hypergraph
independent set partition function, which matches, up to lower order factors, where Glauber dynamics
mixes rapidly [HSZ19]. Lee-Yang zeros will also be the focus of our work, while our approach can be
extended to the study of Fisher zeros around the entire interval of [0, 1].
Indeed, the framework of complex extensions of Markov chain [LWYY25] is a promising starting point
for studying Lee-Yang zeros of CSPs under LLL-like conditions. An obvious obstacle, however, is that CSPs
under LLL-like conditions may have disconnected solution space, ruling out standard Glauber dynamics
Markov chains (see, e.g., [FM11, Section 5] for the case of hypergraph 𝑞-coloring). Existing Markov chain
approaches crucially rely on Glauber dynamics on a projection of the original instance, in which every
step of the Glauber dynamics is (approximately) simulated with a rejection sampling [FHY21, JPV21a]. By
applying the complex extension of Glauber dynamics on the projected CSP instance, one only gains control
on the projected measure, while proving zero-freeness requires lifting it back to the original measure. For
hypergraph 𝑞-colorings, we are able to do both projection and lifting without loss, and also carry out the
analysis of the complex extension of Glauber dynamics on the projection, leading to a zero-free region
around the point 1 of any given color under LLL-like conditions. For more general CSPs like 𝑘-CNF, we
are currently able to show zero-freeness around the point 1 only for a large sub-instance. We leave as an
interesting open problem how to get zero-freeness on the original instance without requiring projection.
1.1. Our results. We formally state our results next. We focus on hypergraph 𝑞-colorings, while we also
remark that our framework in Section 4 is applicable to more general CSPs that are atomic (that is, each
constraint can be violated by at most one assignment to its variables). For general CSPs, we turn them into
atomic CSPs by adding atomic constraints.
Let 𝐻= (𝑉, E) be a 𝑘-uniform hypergraph with maximum degree Δ. A coloring on the vertices of 𝐻
is said to be proper if every hyperedge is not monochromatic. To study Lee-Yang zeros, we introduce a
complex external field on the color 1. The choice of color 1 is arbitrary due to symmetry. Formally, the

1.2. Technical overview. Our starting point for proving zero-freeness is the framework of [LWYY25].
We explain our main techniques in the context of hypergraph 𝑞-coloring in the following.
1.2.1. Zero-freeness via constraint-wise self-reduction. A standard self-reduction can reduce proving zero-

1.2.2. Projection and lifting scheme. Liu et al. [LWYY25] introduced complex extensions of Glauber dynam-
ics as a proxy for similar marginals for hypergraph independent sets. As alluded to in the introduction,
standard Glauber dynamics is not ergodic for hypergraph colorings [FM11, Wig19], and one has to work
with a state-compression scheme instead [FHY21, JPV21a, HSW21]. Specifically, a projection is designed to
compress the domain alphabet into smaller ones so that: a) the Glauber dynamics on the projected alpha-
bet is rapid mixing; b) the projected Glauber dynamics can be lifted back to the original instance through
rejection sampling. Crucially, conditioned on the projected symbols, the CSP formula shatters into small
connected components with high probability. This enables efficient rejection sampling.
To work with complex measures, we start by analyzing a projected complex measure. We consider
the following projection 𝑓, which maps a coloring 𝜎∈[𝑞]𝑉to a color bucket assignment 𝜏= 𝑓(𝜎) ∈
{1▲, 2▲, . . . , 𝐵▲}𝑉, where 𝜏𝑣= 𝑓(𝜎𝑣) for any 𝑣∈𝑉. The first color is projected to the first color bucket
1▲(we use ▲to denote the color bucket). The remaining 𝑞−1 colors are evenly partitioned among the
other 𝐵−1 buckets, so that each bucket (except 1▲) contains approximately the same number of original
colors. Then, the projected measure on {1▲, 2▲, . . . , 𝐵▲}𝑉is given by 𝜓𝐻𝑖(𝜏) = Í

1.2.3. Analyzing the projected measure via complex Glauber dynamics. To bound the right-hand side of (4),
we extend the complex Markov chain framework developed in [LWYY25] to general CSPs. Intuitively, to
analyze a marginal measure 𝜓of an event 𝐴, one analyzes the event 𝐴with respect to the resulting state
of a complex Glauber dynamics instead.

A key insight from [LWYY25] is to use a “percolation” style argument for both establishing the con-

For a “percolation” style argument to work in the complex plane, [LWYY25] formulated the notion of
witness sequence, conditioned on which the complex measure 𝜓GD
𝑇

𝑇

witness) ∈{0, 1}. Intuitively, a witness sequence has two objectives: a) it encodes the “trajectory” of a
complex Markov chain so that the final event 𝜎0 ∈𝐴is independent of the initial state; b) the measure of
the encoding cannot be much larger than the measure of oblivious updates.
To analyze our projected measure𝜓𝐻𝑖, we follow [LWYY25] closely in spirit, but we also have to innovate
in the following aspects.
Decomposition of transition measures: The first step is to carefully decompose transition measures
into oblivious and non-oblivious transitions, so that oblivious ones are close to the true transition measures.
In the context of hypergraph 𝑞-colorings for real probability measures, similar decomposition is known
through local uniformity [GLLZ18, HSW21, FHY21, JPV21a], which roughly means that under local lemma
conditions, local marginals on variables are close to the uniform product distribution. A complex analogue
of local uniformity seems highly non-trivial, as existing arguments rely on LLL [HSS11] on the real line.
Furthermore, in order to introduce external fields to a given color (as opposed to external fields on color
buckets), our state-compression scheme requires designating one special color, under which standard local
uniformity no longer holds even in the real line.
We bypass these difficulties with two observations. The first is to work with “local uniformity” of the
transition measure (as opposed to that of the stationary measure). In the transition measure, the difference
between real-valued or complex-valued external field 𝜆can be bounded in a neighborhood of the interval
[0, 1]. Concretely, given a bucket assignment𝜏∈{1▲, 2▲, . . . , 𝐵▲}𝑉\𝑣, for each 𝑖∈[𝐵], let 𝑋𝑖be the number

[LWYY25] characterized witness sequences in the setting of hypergraph independent sets formally
through the notion of bad component. This naturally generalizes to hypergraph colorings with one special
color. However, the witness sequences characterized by analogous bad components only satisfy part (1)
in Property 1.5. To restore the “zero-one” law, we also need to encode extra information so that combined
with bad components, will play the role of part (2) and (3), which can then be used to establish part (4).
Essentially, the extra encoding only introduces an additional conditioning during the derivation of eq. (7).
Next, we describe some intuitions for how to satisfy part (1) in Property 1.5, and restoring the “zero-one”
law in hypergraph colorings. We recall the relaxed local uniformity, which says that all but the special
color 1▲have approximately equal marginals. So the oblivious transitions would be when trying to update
to a non-special color bucket uniformly at random. We use 𝑟𝑡∈{2▲, 3▲, . . . , 𝐵▲, ⊥} to denote the result of
the oblivious transition at time 𝑡, where ⊥is a placeholder indicating that the oblivious transition alone at
time 𝑡cannot fully determine the update value, and a non-oblivious update may be needed.
To see how to satisfy part (1) of Property 1.5, we consider a transition step at time 𝑡with a vertex
𝑣to update, and we reveal the oblivious color bucket assignments of 𝑟𝑠for all neighboring vertices of
𝑣at time 𝑠just prior to 𝑡. If all hyperedges containing 𝑣are not monochromatic by these 𝑟𝑠, then we
know the update at time 𝑡is independent of the initial state. In fact, conditioned on these 𝑟𝑠, since all the
neighboring constraints are already satisfied, the complex measure factorizes (see Observation 4.15 for a
formal statement). Similarly, for an update at time 𝑡, if for all hyperedges containing 𝑣, their last updates
are independent of the initial state, then we know the update at time𝑡is also independent of the initial state.
By revealing the oblivious color bucket assignments backwards in time, this forms a percolation on the
space-time diagram, and its structure is formally captured by a bad component Cbad (see Definition 4.14).
Intuitively, a bad component is “small” exactly when the corresponding 𝑟𝑡’s is a witness sequence (that is,
satisfying part (1)).
Zero-one law: Compared to [LWYY25], there are two complications in trying to restore a “zero-one”
law. Firstly, bad component itself does not provide enough information for general CSP, and 𝜓GD
𝑇
(𝜎0 ∈
𝐴∧Cbad) is still a highly non-trivial complex measure; secondly, the fact that we have to work with a
projected measure also leads to much more sophisticated events 𝐴(corresponding to a bad cluster during
projection-lifting), for which we need to restore the “zero-one” law. Our idea is to also encode the actual
updated values within a bad component. Let BadTS  Cbad denote the update times involved in the bad
7


## Page 9


component Cbad, and 𝒐BadTS(Cbad) denote the updated values in BadTS  Cbad. We show that given Cbad
and 𝒐BadTS(Cbad), we can still recover a zero-one law (Lemma 4.16).
Then, to satisfy part (3) in Property 1.5, we also need a much more refined analysis compared to [LWYY25]
in order to have room for both the more complicated events 𝐴(which can depend on an unbounded number
of variables) and the extra encoding.
We restore part (4) in Property 1.5 by combining part (1), (2) and (3) to show that contributions from
“large” bad components diminish to 0 as 𝑇→∞. This part of the property is formalized as Condition 3.9,
which we formally verify in Section 4.2.4.
1.2.4. CLTs, LCLTs and concentration inequalities under LLL-like conditions. Given our zero-freeness, cen-
tral limit theorems follow from [MS24] together with a lower bound on the variance. By the definition of
variance, it suffices to show a lower bound of variance on each vertex and a lower bound on the covariance
(see eq. (50)). The first lower bound follows from local uniformity (Lemma C.4 and corollary C.5). For the
second one, we make use of a recent breakthrough in sampling LLL [WY24] to bound the total influence
(Lemma C.6) which leads to a lower bound of the covariance (see eq. (51)). In Section D, following the
spirit of [JPSS22], we boost CLTs into LCLTs through a Fourier inversion (see Lemma D.5). We extend
their analysis to general CSPs for constant 𝜆.
Our simple Chebyshev-type inequality consists of two components: a lower bound on the expectation
and an upper bound on the variance. The lower bound on expectation follows from local uniformity, while
the upper bound again makes use of a recent breakthrough in sampling LLL [WY24]. Among others, they
showed a constant total influence bound with respect to a constraint. Translating this to a bound with
respect to a variable only loses factors of 𝑘, which can then be used to bound the covariances between
variables. We also lose a factor of 𝑞due to pinning on vertices introduced in the covariances calculation.
This leads to a Chebyshev inequality under 𝑝𝐷2+𝑜𝑞(1) ≲1/𝑞.

2.3. Glauber dynamics: random scan and systematic scan. We recall the standard Glauber dynamics.
Let 𝜇be a distribution on 𝑸with 𝑉= {𝑣1, 𝑣2, . . . , 𝑣𝑛}. The Glauber dynamics is a canonical construction
of Markov chains with stationary distribution 𝜇. Starting from an initial state 𝑋0 ∈𝑸with 𝜇(𝑋0) ≠0, the
chain proceeds as follows at each step 𝑡:
• pick a variable 𝑣∈𝑉uniformly at random and set 𝑋𝑡(𝑢) = 𝑋𝑡−1(𝑢) for all 𝑢≠𝑣;
• update 𝑋𝑡(𝑣) by sampling from the distribution 𝜇𝑋𝑡−1(𝑉\{𝑣})

The systematic scan Glauber dynamics is a variant of the standard Glauber dynamics. In each step,
instead of updating a variable at random, it updates them in a canonical order. Specifically, at each step 𝑡,
we choose the variable 𝑣= 𝑣𝑖(𝑡), where
𝑖(𝑡) ≜(𝑡
mod 𝑛) + 1.

Then 𝑋𝑡−1 is updated to 𝑋𝑡using the same rule as in Glauber dynamics, based on the chosen 𝑣.
The Glauber dynamics is well known to be both aperiodic and reversible with respect to 𝜇. The system-
atic scan Glauber dynamics is not time-homogeneous, as variables are accessed in a cyclic order. However,
by bundling 𝑛consecutive updates, we obtain a time-homogeneous Markov chain that is aperiodic and re-
versible.

2.6. 2-tree. We also need the notion of 2-trees [Alo91]. Given a graph 𝐺= (𝑉, 𝐸), its square graph
𝐺2 = (𝑉, 𝐸2) has the same vertex set, while an edge (𝑢, 𝑣) ∈𝐸2 if and only if 1 ≤dist𝐺(𝑢, 𝑣) ≤2.
Definition 2.4 (2-tree). Let 𝐺= (𝑉, 𝐸) be a graph. A set of vertices 𝑇⊆𝑉is called a 2-tree of 𝐺, if
• for any 𝑢, 𝑣∈𝑇, dist𝐺(𝑢, 𝑣) ≥2, and
• 𝑇is connected on 𝐺2.
Intuitively, a 2-tree is an independent set that does not spread far away. We can construct a large 2-tree
in any connected graph as follows.
Definition 2.5 (Construction of a maximal 2-tree [JPV21b, Lemma 4.5]). Let 𝐺= (𝑉, 𝐸) be a connected
graph of maximum degree 𝐷and 𝑣∈𝑉. We can deterministically construct a 2-tree 𝑇of 𝑉containing 𝑣
such that |𝑇| ≥⌊|𝑉|/(𝐷+ 1)⌋as follows:
• order the vertices in 𝑉in the lexicographical order. Start with 𝑇= {𝑣} and 𝑈= 𝑉\ 𝑁+(𝑣), where
𝑁+(𝑣) ≜𝑁(𝑣) ∪{𝑣} and 𝑁(𝑣) ≜{𝑢∈𝑉| (𝑢, 𝑣) ∈𝐸} ;
• repeat until 𝑈= ∅: let 𝑢be the vertex in 𝑈with the smallest distance to 𝑇, with ties broken by the
order on 𝑉. Set 𝑇←𝑇∪{𝑢} and 𝑈←𝑈\ 𝑁+(𝑢).
The following lemma bounds the number of subtrees and 2-trees of a certain size containing a given
vertex, respectively.

3. ContRolling maRginal measuRes via analytic peRcolation
A crucial step in establishing zero-freeness is to bound the norm of certain complex marginal measures.
In this section, we review the theory of complex Markov chains developed in [LWYY25], which provides
a novel tool for analyzing complex normalized measures via complex Markov chains. We demonstrate
how to analyze the convergence of complex Markov chains (Lemma 3.10) and how to bound the norm of
marginal measures (Lemma 3.11).
For cases where the theory of complex Markov chains breaks down—such as when the support set
is disconnected—we propose a projection-lifting scheme. In this approach, we first analyze a “projected
complex normalized measure” using the complex Markov chain theory, and then lift the result back to the
original complex normalized measure via the law of total measure.
3.1. Projection-lifting scheme. To introduce our projection-lifting scheme, we first define the projected
measure. Let 𝚺= (Σ𝑣)𝑣∈𝑉where for any 𝑣∈𝑉, Σ𝑣is a finite alphabet. Let 𝒇= (𝑓𝑣)𝑣∈𝑉be a series
of projections where 𝑓𝑣: 𝑄𝑣→Σ𝑣. We naturally interpret 𝒇as a function, for any 𝜎∈𝑸, we have
𝒇(𝜎) = (𝑓𝑣(𝜎𝑣))𝑣∈𝑉. We refer to 𝚺as the projected alphabet or projected domain. The projected measure 𝜓
on the projected alphabet 𝚺is defined as:

complex marginal measures. Let 𝐴⊆𝑸be an event. Now we demonstrate how to upper bound |𝜇(𝐴)|

We first define a series of events B1, B2, . . . , B𝑚, where for each 𝑖≤𝑚, B𝑖⊆𝚺. We remark that

3.2. Complex Extensions of Markov Chains. We review the notion of complex extension of Markov
chains from [LWYY25], where we make the modifications in this paper: the Glauber dynamics is applied
only to a projected measure.

4. ZeRo-fReeness foR CSP foRmulas
In this section, we apply our decomposition scheme (Definition 3.7) to CSP formulas and prove a suf-
ficient condition for zero-freeness (Theorem 4.6). We remark that we work with general CSPs, but Con-
dition 4.4 implicitly transforms any general CSPs into atomic CSPs. To establish zero-freeness, we start
with a constraint-wise self-reduction, which reduces zero-freeness to analyzing the norm of a complex
marginal measure. Then we apply our projection-lifting scheme (Section 3.1) to analyze a projected mea-
sure constructed by the standard state-compression scheme. The state-compression scheme and its special
case “mark/unmark” method are widely used for sampling satisfying assignments of CSP formulas [Moi19,
GLLZ18, FGYZ21a, FHY21, JPV21a, JPV21b, HSW21, GGGHP22, HWY22, HWY23a, HWY23b, CMM23].
For the real case, the projected distribution has many good properties such as the local uniformity which
can be used to prove the rapid mixing of Markov chains on the projected distribution and construct local
15


## Page 17


samplers. For our complex case, we use our decomposition scheme (Definition 3.7) to analyze the complex
systematic scan Glauber dynamics on the projected measure. Then we lift the analysis for the projected
measure back to the original measure and bound the norm of a complex marginal measure.
We start by defining the framework of the state-compression scheme.

Here we do not aim to provide a zero-free condition for general CSP formulas. We only focus on (𝑘, Δ)-
CSP formulas and provide a sufficient condition for our zero-freeness (Theorem 4.6). We first introduce
some useful notations. Recall that for any constraint 𝑐∈𝐶, we use vbl(𝑐) to denote the variables that
𝑐depends on and we use 𝑐−1(False) to denote the set of partial assignments on vbl(𝑐) that violate the
constraint 𝑐. For any assignment 𝜎∈𝚺under the projection, and any constraint 𝑐, recall that we say 𝑐is
satisfied by 𝜎iff for any violating assignment 𝜏∈𝑐−1 (False), there exists 𝑣∈vbl(𝑐) such that 𝑓𝑣(𝜏𝑣) ≠𝜎𝑣.
This means that any original assignment whose projected symbol is 𝜎satisfies the constraint 𝑐. Let Λ ⊆𝑉.
For an assignment 𝜎∈𝚺Λ under the projection, we say 𝜎is an admissible partial assignment under the
projection, if its projected measure is not 0, i.e.,𝜓(𝜎) ≠0. And we say 𝜎is an extendable partial assignment
under the projection, if it can be extended into an admissible projected assignment.

4.1. Proof of the induction step. In this subsection, we prove Lemma 4.5. For simplicity, we consider
a (𝑘, Δ)-CSP formula Φ = (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶) with an extra constraint 𝑐∗such that (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶∪{𝑐∗}) is also a (𝑘, Δ)-

𝑍(Φ, 𝜆,𝒇, 1▲) ≠0. Let 𝜇= 𝜇(Φ, 𝜆,𝒇, 1▲) be the Gibbs distribution. It suffices to show |𝜇(𝑐∗is violated)| < 1.
Next, we use our projection-lifting scheme (Condition 3.1) to bound this norm. Recall that in our projection-
lifting scheme, we need to design events with respect to the projected measure 𝜓such that fixing the con-
tributions from complex external fields (Condition 3.1). Here we use pinnings to fix those contributions,
and we use the following combinatorial structures to construct these events and later we use the complex
Markov chain to bound the norm of each event. We remark that this argument can also be seen as a
complex analogue of the lazy perfect sampling scheme [AJ22, FGW+23].
Recall that for any assignment 𝜎∈𝚺under the projection, and any constraint 𝑐, we say 𝑐is satisfied by
𝜎iff for any violating assignment 𝜏∈𝑐−1 (False) there exists 𝑣∈vbl(𝑐) such that 𝑓𝑣(𝜏𝑣) ≠𝜎𝑣. This means
that any original assignment whose projected symbol is 𝜎satisfies the constraint 𝑐. Otherwise, we say 𝑐
is not satisfied by 𝜎.
Definition 4.7 (Bad constraint, bad cluster). Let Φ = (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶) be a CSP formula, with the projection 𝒇
in the state-compression scheme (recall Definition 4.1), and the complex external field 𝜆on the symbol 1▲
(Definition 4.2).
Let 𝑐∗be an additional constraint. Given an assignment 𝜎∈𝚺𝑉under the projection, we define the
following structures on the dependency graph 𝐺dep
Φ′ of Φ′ = (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶∪{𝑐∗}).

containing the additional constraint 𝑐∗. If 𝑐∗∉𝐸bad, then we define Sbad = ∅.
Now, we use bad clusters to define the events in our projection-lifting scheme (Section 3.1) and note
that if “𝑐∗is violated”, then the bad cluster must not be ∅. By the above definition, Condition 3.1-(1) and

Lemma 4.8. For any partial assignment 𝜏∈𝚺vbl(Sbad) under the projection, it holds that
(1) if 𝜇(Sbad = S ∧𝜏) = 0, then 𝜇(𝑐∗is violated ∧Sbad = S ∧𝜏) = 0,

the projection. We denote the partition function of Φ′ as 𝑍′.
For the contributions from vbl(S), by the fact that S∗are all satisfied, we only need to consider the
constraints in S. Note that from 𝜏and our complex extension of CSP formulas (Definition 4.2), we can

For any 𝑢∈𝑉and integer 𝑡, we denote by pred𝑢(𝑡) the last time before 𝑡at which 𝑢is updated, i.e.

inition 4.14). Recall the 𝑜𝑡’s in our complex systematic scan Glauber dynamics (Algorithm 2), and recall

value of 𝜎0(𝑣). Then we provide two technical lemmas which show the convergence and give the upper
bounds of norm of marginals measure. Finally, we use these two technical lemmas to prove Lemma 4.9.
We start with a lemma about the determination of Sbad(𝜎0) and the assignment involved in it.

5. Applications
In this section, we apply the framework in Section 4 and consider the zero-freeness of hypergraph
𝑞-coloring and (𝑘, Δ)-CNF formulas, proving Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 5.15 respectively.
5.1. Hypergraph 𝑞-coloring with one special color. In this subsection, we consider the complex ex-
tension of hypergraph 𝑞-coloring with a complex external field on the color 1. We show that under certain
conditions, the complex partition function is zero-free. We formally define the partition function as follows.

The remaining subsection is devoted to the proof of Lemma 5.5. Let Φ be the CSP formula that en-
codes the hypergraph 𝑞-coloring on the hypergraph 𝐻. Suppose Condition 5.4 holds. Note that under the
constraint-wise self-reduction, new CSP formulas are also (𝑘, Δ)-CSP formulas satisfying Condition 5.4
and they also encode hypergraph 𝑞-colorings for different hypergraphs. Hence, we prove a stronger result
that for any CSP formula encoding a hypergraph 𝑞-coloring, if Condition 5.4 holds for the formula, then
Condition 4.4 holds. Thus, we have that Φ1, Φ2, . . . , Φ𝑚satisfy Condition 4.4.
We first verify Condition 4.4-(1).

5.2. (𝑘, Δ)-CNF formulas. In this subsection, we consider the zero-freeness of (𝑘, Δ)-CNF formulas. A
(𝑘, Δ)-CNF is a formula Φ = (𝑉, {0, 1}𝑉,𝐶) where each clause has exactly 𝑘variables and each variable
belongs to at most Δ clauses. We use 0 to denote “False” and 1 to denote “True”. Let ΩΦ ⊆{0, 1}𝑉be the
set of solutions to the formula Φ. For a set of variables M ⊆𝑉, we introduce the complex external field
on these variables M. We define the partition function formally.

We remark that unlike the zero-free region of the hypergraph 𝑞coloring, the zero-free regime of the
CNF formula does not contain 0. In the remaining part of this subsection, we prove Theorem 5.15. We use
Lemma 5.18 to verify the Condition 4.4 in Theorem 4.6. We first introduce some useful notations.
The subset M is constructed by the state-compression scheme which is also known as the “mark/unmark”
method for CNF formulas. It plays a very important role in sampling/counting Lovász local lemma [Moi19,
GLLZ18, FGYZ21a, FHY21, JPV21a, JPV21b, HSW21, GGGHP22, HWY22, HWY23a, HWY23b, CMM23].
We divide the variables into two parts: marked variables M and unmarked variables 𝑉\ M, and we add
the complex external field 𝜆on marked variables M.

Appendix B. ConcentRation ineality foR CSP foRmulas
In this section, we derive a concentration inequality on the number of variables taking the value 1 for
atomic CSP formulas through Chebyshev’s inequality and prove Theorem 1.4.
To use Chebyshev’s inequality, it suffices to derive an lower bound of the expectation and an upper
bound for the variance. For the lower bound of the expectation, we utilize the local uniformity. For the
variance, by the definition of variance, we consider the per-variable variances and covariances (see eq. (49)).
For the per-variable variances, we again utilize the local uniformity. For the covariances, we employ the
recursive coupling from [WY24] to upper bound the total influence.
Let Φ = (𝑉, [𝑞]𝑉,𝐶) be an (𝑘, Δ)-CSP formula. Let 𝜆> 0 be the external field on the value 1, and let the
partition function be 𝑍= 𝑍(Φ, 𝜆) = Í

𝜎∼𝜇. For any 𝑣∈𝑉, let X𝑣= I[𝜎𝑣= 1] be a random variable. Let X = Í

Appendix D. Local centRal limit theoRem
In this section, we derive a local central limit theorem for CSP formulas from the central limit theorem.
As an application, we prove a local CLT for hypergraph coloring with one special color in Section D.1. We
follow the same high-level ideas in [JPSS22] (See also [LWYY25]). We remark that in [JPSS22, LWYY25],
the external field 𝜆need not be a constant and can depend on |𝑉|. This allows them to derive an algorithm
for counting fix-sized independent sets. For general CSP formulas such as hypergraph colorings, handling
𝜆that are arbitrarily small seems to require new ideas, and we focus on constant external field 𝜆in this
work.
Let Φ = (𝑉, [𝑞]𝑉,𝐶) be an atomic (𝑘, Δ)-CSP formula. Our local CLT concerns Φ under projection.
Again, we consider the state-compression scheme (Definition 4.1) with the projection 𝒇= (𝑓𝑣)𝑣∈𝑉where
𝑓𝑣: [𝑞] →Σ𝑣. Let 𝜇be the Gibbs distribution on ΩΦ with the external field 𝜆on the projected symbol 1▲
(Definition 4.2 with 𝜆is a real number). We consider the local CLT on the number of variables assigned
the projected symbol 1▲. Let 𝜎∼𝜇following 𝜇, and let 𝑌𝑣be the random variable that indicates whether
the projected symbol of 𝑣is 1▲; i.e., if 𝜎𝑣∈𝑓−1

Appendix E. FisheR zeRos
In this section, we derive the Fisher zeros for CSP formulas. Let Φ = (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶) be a (𝑘, Δ)-CSP formula
and let 𝛽∈C. We define the partition function with respect to 𝛽as
𝑍fs = 𝑍fs(Φ, 𝛽) ≜

(55)
We remark that 𝛽can be seen as a “penalty” when a constraint is violated. Two interesting points are
𝛽= 0, which refers to the uniform distribution over all satisfying assignment, and 𝛽= 1 which refers to
the simple product distribution.
To obtain zero-freeness in 𝛽, we follow a similar reduction in [LWYY25, Section 6] that reduces the
Fisher zeros into Lee-Yang zeros (of another CSP) by introducing new variables. Then we apply the frame-
work in Section 4, specifically, we verify Condition 4.4 and apply Theorem 4.6.
We first provide the reduction. We remark that when 𝛽= 1, the partition function 𝑍fs(Φ, 1) = |𝑸| which
is trivially not zero. Hence, we focus on the case 𝛽≠1.
Definition E.1 (Reduction from the Fisher zeros to Lee-Yang zeros). Given a CSP formula Φ = (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶)
with a complex number 𝛽∈C \ {1}, we construct a new CSP formula Φ′ = (𝑉′, 𝑸′,𝐶′) and a new complex
number 𝜆=

• for any constraint 𝑐∈𝐶, we construct a new constraint 𝑐′ and add a new variable 𝑣𝑐′ whose domain
is {0, 1}. Let 𝜏= 𝑐−1(False) be the violating partial assignment of the constraint 𝑐. Then we set
𝜏𝑣𝑐′ = 0 and 𝑐′−1(False) = 𝜏. Let 𝐶′ = {𝑐′ | 𝑐∈𝐶}.
• Let 𝑉′ = 𝑉∪{𝑣𝑐′ | 𝑐′ ∈𝐶′}.
We call variables in 𝑉as original variables and variables in 𝑉′ \ 𝑉as new variables. Recall that ΩΦ′ is
the set of all satisfying assignments of the CSP formula Φ′. Let 𝑍ly = 𝑍ly(Φ′, 𝜆) be the partition function
with respect to Lee-Yang zeros defined as

Lemma E.2 (Properties of the reduction). Let Φ = (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶) be a (𝑘, Δ)-CSP formula and 𝛽∈C \ {1} be a
complex number. Let (Φ′, 𝜆) = red(Φ, 𝛽). Then,
(1) Φ′ is a (𝑘+ 1, Δ)-CSP formula.
(2) 𝑍fs(Φ, 𝛽) = (1 −𝛽)|𝐶| · 𝑍ly(Φ′, 𝜆).
Proof. For item 1, note that in Definition E.1, we introduce a new variable for each constraint and this new
variable only belongs to this constraint. Hence, item 1 follows.
We then prove item 2. For any logical expression 𝑃, we define the Iverson bracket [𝑃] = 1 if 𝑃is true,
otherwise [𝑃] = 0.
Recall eq. (56). Also recall that 𝑉is the original variable set of Φ. For the reduced CSP formula Φ′. we
enumerate partial assignments on the original variables 𝑉. If a constraint 𝑐′ is already satisfied by the

By the above lemma, to obtain the zero-freeness, it suffices to show that 𝑍ly(Φ′, 𝜆) ≠0. We provide the
next theorem by leveraging the framework in Section 4.
Theorem E.3 (Zero-freeness for Fisher zeros). Let Φ = (𝑉, 𝑸,𝐶) be a (𝑘, Δ)-CSP formula and 𝛽∈C \ {1}
be a complex number. Let (Φ′, 𝜆) = red(Φ, 𝛽) be constructed by Definition E.1 where Φ′ = (𝑉′, 𝑸′,𝐶′). Write

Proof. By Lemma E.2, it holds that 𝑍fs(Φ, 𝛽) = (1 −𝛽)|𝐶| · 𝑍ly(Φ′, 𝜆). It suffices to show 𝑍ly(Φ′, 𝜆) ≠0.
Recall the complex extension of CSP formulas Definition 4.2. Combined with item 1 and item 2, we have
𝑍ly(Φ′, 𝜆) = 𝑍(Φ′, 𝜆,𝒇, 1▲).
Finally, by Theorem 4.6 and item 3, we have 𝑍(Φ′, 𝜆,𝒇, 1▲) ≠0. Hence, this theorem follows.
□
By running similar calculations for 𝑍fs(Φ, 𝛽) as in Section 5.1, one can show that under the condition

interpolation method [Bar16, PR17, LSS17]. We remark that currently the best FPTAS [WY24], which
is based on the recursive coupling and Moitra’s LP approach, works for 𝑘≥8 and 𝑞≥70Δ
